How AgentFlo built AI sales agents with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore – Part 2

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/how-agentflo-built-ai-sales-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-part-2/📅 2026-08-21 10:01 UTC

<p>If you’re building AI agents for commerce at scale, you face two critical challenges: handling unpredictable traffic spikes and ensuring your agents can be trusted with real customer transactions.</p> <p>This post shows how AgentFlo solved these challenges using <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</a> and AWS serverless architecture. You learn the architectural patterns behind their reliability and trust frameworks, see the measurable business results (including +12% net revenue uplift based on early deployment data), and explore their roadmap for voice agents and server-side tool execution.</p> <p>This is Part 2 of a two-part series. <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/how-agentflo-built-ai-sales-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-part-1/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Part 1</a> covers velocity, standardization, and scalability.</p> <h2 id="pillar-4-trust-guardrails-for-autonomous-commercial-action-and-real-time-visibility-into-agent-operations">Pillar 4: Trust: guardrails for autonomous commercial action and real-time visibility into agent operations</h2> <p>AgentFlo enforces trust at every layer of the stack, from pre-request filtering to post-response privacy controls, so merchants can deploy autonomous agents with confidence.</p> <h3 id="the-challenge">The challenge</h3> <p>Enterprise customers won’t deploy autonomous agents unless they can trust them. An agent in production can’t expose sensitive data, offer unauthorized discounts, or access another customer’s information. The system also prevents price hallucination, unauthorized tool calls, opt-out violations, and credential exposure.</p> <p>Merchants also need fine-grained control over who can interact with their AI agents and what data each segment can access. Enterprise customers require restricted access; B2C businesses need open access for broader reach. Without identity-based controls, deploying customer-facing AI is a non-starter.</p> <h3 id="defense-in-depth">Defense in depth</h3> <p>In AgentFlo, trust isn’t only about safe responses. It’s about safe action. Agents can create carts, place orders, apply discounts, access customer data, and interact with backend systems, so policy enforcement must sit outside the model’s reasoning loop. The model proposes. Deterministic policy decides.</p> <p>AgentFlo applies trust controls across the full agent lifecycle: before the model sees the request, during tool execution, and after the model generates a response.</p> <h3 id="three-layer-guardrails">Three-layer guardrails</h3> <p>When users deploy an agent from the AgentFlo Portal, security enforcement happens at three stages.</p> <p>First, the AWS Fargate layer detects prompt injection and handles opt-outs before requests reach the agent. WhatsApp messages are authenticated using phone numbers as unique identifiers. Enterprise customers like EBM restrict access to authorized users, while restaurant deployments stay open for broader reach.</p> <p>Next, the <strong>AgentCore layer</strong> verifies identity and enforces order locks during tool execution. AgentCore Gateway, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, enforces policies that prevent sales agents from accessing customer support tools. Cedar policies enforce business rules like maximum discount percentages independently of the model’s reasoning. Cedar is an open-source policy language developed by AWS for fine-grained, verifiable authorization decisions. Additionally, Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore integrates with <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock Guardrails</a>, so Cedar policies can invoke configurable safeguards for prompt attack detection, content filtering, and sensitive information blocking directly at the gateway boundary.</p> <p>Finally, <strong>post-turn privacy filters</strong> screen outputs to block inadvertent token disclosure and unverified price claims before customers see responses. Secrets are managed through <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/secrets-manager/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Secrets Manager</a> with OIDC (OpenID Connect)-authenticated continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) pipelines. No credentials are stored in agent code.</p> <h3 id="infrastructure-security">Infrastructure security</h3> <p>Trust at the application layer requires sound underlying infrastructure. AgentFlo combines the built-in isolation of AgentCore with application-level controls:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Session isolation</strong>: Session isolation through AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, which provides complete separation between merchants’ agent sessions through dedicated microVMs.</li> <li><strong>AgentCore Gateway policies</strong>: Fine-grained Cedar policies control which agents can access which tools and data, enforced deterministically regardless of model reasoning.</li> <li><strong><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/?nc1=f_m" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)</a>-based access control</strong>: Fine-grained permissions for agent-to-service communication.</li> <li><strong><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/vpc/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC)</a> integration</strong>: Agent sessions operate within AgentFlo’s VPC with domain-level network restrictions, so agents only communicate with approved endpoints.</li> <li><strong>Compliance</strong>: Data residency controls and audit trails for regulatory requirements across multiple jurisdictions.</li> </ul> <h3 id="observability">Observability</h3> <p>Trust requires visibility. Merchants need to see what their agents are doing in real time, not only after something goes wrong. AgentFlo uses AgentCore Observability, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, to provide end-to-end tracing of every agent interaction, from initial request through tool execution to final response.</p> <p>AgentCore Observability captures structured traces for each agent turn, including model latency, tool invocation sequences, token usage, and error rates. These traces flow into Amazon CloudWatch, where AgentFlo builds dashboards showing active sessions, response times, and tool call patterns. Full request-to-response traces enable trace-level debugging. The system tracks P50/P95 latencies and throughput across agent types, alerting merchants when behavior deviates from baselines. Cost attribution provides per-merchant, per-agent breakdowns tied to specific conversations.</p> <h3 id="results">Results</h3> <p>Together, observability and trust give enterprises confidence to deploy autonomous agents at scale. Merchants benefit from safer execution, stronger compliance across jurisdictions, and controlled access to tools and data at the session level.</p> <h2 id="pillar-5-reliability-a-data-foundation-that-keeps-agents-grounded-in-reliable-data">Pillar 5: Reliability: a data foundation that keeps agents grounded in reliable data</h2> <p>Reliable agents need reliable data. AgentFlo grounds every agent action in verified, current information through stateful sessions, merchant knowledge bases, and semantic product discovery.</p> <h3 id="the-challenge-1">The challenge</h3> <p>Enterprise customers won’t trust AI agents that forget context, hallucinate product details, or operate on stale data. An agent that quotes the wrong price, forgets a customer’s earlier request, or recommends discontinued products destroys confidence instantly. The system must make sure every agent action is grounded in verified, current information, from product catalogs and pricing to conversation history and business rules.</p> <p>Merchants also need their agents to maintain continuity across long customer journeys, access up-to-date business-specific knowledge, and surface products through natural language, all without manual intervention or prompt engineering.</p> <h3 id="data-architecture-overview">Data architecture overview</h3> <p>In AgentFlo, reliability isn’t only about accurate responses. It’s about accurate action grounded in verified data. Agents retrieve product information, manage carts, and complete transactions, so every data source must be authoritative and current. The model reasons. Structured data decides.</p> <p>AgentFlo applies data reliability controls across three layers: stateful conversation management through <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon DynamoDB</a>, merchant-specific knowledge through <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases</a>, and semantic product discovery through vector embeddings in Amazon <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/features/vectors/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">S3 Vector</a>.</p> <h3 id="state-management-architecture">State management architecture</h3> <p>A real sales journey can span 8 hours or 3 days. A customer might ask about a product in the morning, compare options at lunch, and complete the purchase that evening. The agent must remember context across all turns and maintain state, so customers don’t need to start over.</p> <p>AgentCore runtime and Amazon DynamoDB manage this context storage. The agent replays relevant history, loads context based on intent, and continues transactions safely.</p> <p>Each agent needs to be stateful (remembering earlier interactions), autonomous (deciding next steps without human intervention), and safe (operating within business rules without exposing sensitive data).</p> <p>The two-table DynamoDB design covers all three requirements: session continuity through conversation replay, autonomous context loading based on detected intent, and data integrity through structured ground-truth storage that the model can’t hallucinate over.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/08/11/ARCHBLOG-1672-1.png" alt="Diagram of the per-message conversation flow between AgentCore runtime and the DynamoDB session and cart tables" width="800"> <p class="wp-caption-text"> <br> Figure 1: Per-message conversation flow. AgentCore runtime loads the last 15 messages from the DynamoDB Session Table at the start of each turn. The Cart Table is loaded on demand only when intent detection flags the request as cart-related, preventing the model from generating incorrect prices and quantities. </p> </div> <h3 id="knowledge-base-system">Knowledge base system</h3> <p>Merchants upload business-specific data (restaurant menus, clinic policies, product specifications, promotion calendars) into Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases backed by Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3). Agents automatically retrieve and reason over this merchant-specific content. Responses stay grounded in accurate, up-to-date business information without merchants writing a single prompt.</p> <h3 id="semantic-search-and-vector-retrieval">Semantic search and vector retrieval</h3> <p>AgentFlo improves product discovery by giving every product in its <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/rds/aurora/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Aurora database</a> a lightweight vector embedding. Customers can find products by name or description.</p> <p>AgentFlo also generates extra searchable tags for each product automatically, and merchants can add their own. The result: phrases like “the pink one,” “the smallest one,” “the new one,” or “the chocolate with the golden wrapper” all map to the right product. Customers ask for things naturally, and the platform finds what they’re looking for.</p> <h3 id="observability-and-billing">Observability and billing</h3> <p>The system captures all message interactions through <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/firehose/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Data Firehose</a> to <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon S3</a>, so merchants can track cost per conversation and compare those costs against sales revenue. This pipeline shows merchants the return on investment (ROI) of their agent deployments and provides the data foundation for continuous agent improvement.</p> <h3 id="results-1">Results</h3> <p>The data architecture helps minimize context loss in conversations across multi-day customer journeys, with grounded responses that eliminate price and product hallucination. Merchants benefit from natural language product discovery without keyword dependency and merchant-specific knowledge retrieval without prompt engineering. Full cost visibility and ROI attribution per agent deployment give merchants clear measurement of platform value.</p> <h2 id="business-impact-measurable-results-across-the-customer-lifecycle">Business impact: measurable results across the customer lifecycle</h2> <p>Salesflo’s solution, powered by <a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Strands Agents SDK</a> and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, delivered measurable improvements across the customer lifecycle:</p> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Metric</strong></td> <td><strong>Improvement</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Net revenue uplift</td> <td>+12%</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Customer engagement</td> <td>+40%</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Conversion rate</td> <td>+15%</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Average order value</td> <td>+8%</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Customer reactivation</td> <td>+20%</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <p>AgentFlo generated these results by comparing agent-assisted customer journeys against a control group over a 90-day early deployment period.</p> <p><em>Note: The foundation models referenced in this post are available in select AWS Regions. For the latest information on model availability, see <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Supported Regions and models for Amazon Bedrock</a>.</em></p> <h3 id="the-compounding-effect-at-scale">The compounding effect at scale</h3> <p>At AgentFlo’s scale, the impact is significant. With $300 billion in annual transacted value flowing through the Salesflo solution (based on platform transaction data), even single-digit percentage improvements translate to billions in incremental revenue for merchants.</p> <h3 id="operational-improvements">Operational improvements</h3> <p>Beyond the metrics, merchants see operational improvements:</p> <ul> <li><strong>24/7 coverage</strong> — AI agents engage customers at any hour, in any time zone, across any channel.</li> <li><strong>Consistent quality</strong> — Every customer interaction follows best-practice selling methodologies without the variability of human agents.</li> <li><strong>Scalable personalization</strong> — Thousands of concurrent agent sessions, each maintaining unique context per customer.</li> <li><strong>Rapid merchant onboarding</strong> — New merchants go live with customized AI agents in days, not months, through the self-service configuration platform.</li> </ul> <h2 id="whats-next-voice-server-side-execution-and-integration-expansion">What’s next: voice, server-side execution, and integration expansion</h2> <p>AgentFlo is actively extending the platform along three directions, each at a different stage of maturity.</p> <h3 id="real-time-voice-agents-in-pilot">Real-time voice agents (in pilot)</h3> <p>AgentFlo already supports voice within WhatsApp. Incoming voice notes go through a two-pass transcription process: a raw initial pass, followed by domain-aware correction that fuzzy-matches text against the live product catalog. The second pass catches brand names and SKUs even when partially misheard, across more than 90 supported languages. For outbound audio, merchants pick from multiple Text-to-Speech (TTS) providers per deployment.</p> <p>Because Speech-to-Text (STT), reasoning on AgentCore runtime, and TTS are fully independent components, any one can be swapped without disrupting the rest of the pipeline.</p> <p><strong>The next step: BidiAgent</strong>. The next evolution is real-time voice agents built on the Strands SDK BidiAgent and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/03/amazon-bedrock-webrtc/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock AgentCore WebRTC</a> support. BidiAgent supports bidirectional audio streaming, natural interruptions, and concurrent tool execution. The agent can check inventory or apply a discount while continuing to listen and respond to the customer in the same call.</p> <p>The AgentCore WebRTC protocol and Amazon Kinesis Video Streams handle peer-to-peer transport for mobile and browser interactions without requiring relay infrastructure. This pushes AgentFlo beyond text messaging into proactive outbound calls and high-value B2B sales, where real-time conversation is essential for building trust.</p> <h3 id="server-side-tool-execution-under-development"><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/02/amazon-bedrock-server-side-tool-execution-agentcore-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Server-side tool execution</a> (under development)</h3> <p>AgentFlo is experimenting with server-side tool execution in Amazon Bedrock, which removes client-side orchestration entirely.</p> <p>Traditional orchestration loops between model and tools repeatedly (model → execute → send result → repeat). With server-side execution, the agent makes a single API call to the Amazon Bedrock Responses API with an AgentCore Gateway Amazon Resource Name (ARN). The model then autonomously discovers, invokes, and processes tools through the Gateway Model Context Protocol (MCP) interface, all inside AWS infrastructure with no roundtrips back to the client.</p> <p>Here’s what that one API call looks like:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-python">from openai import OpenAI # OPENAI_BASE_URL = https://bedrock-mantle.us-west-2.api.aws/v1 # OPENAI_API_KEY = &lt;Amazon Bedrock API key&gt; client = OpenAI() response = client.responses.create( model="openai.gpt-oss-120b", stream=True, background=False, store=False, input=[ { "type": "message", "role": "user", "content": [{"type": "input_text", "text": user_message}], } ], tools=[ { "type": "mcp", "server_label": "agentflo_gateway", "connector_id": GATEWAY_ARN, # arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:...:gateway/... "server_description": "AgentFlo commerce tools (cart, catalog, knowledge base)", "require_approval": "never", }, ], )</code></pre> </div> <p><em>Note: Model availability varies by AWS Region. The model and endpoint shown in this example may not be available in all Regions. See <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Supported Regions and models for Amazon Bedrock</a> for current availability.</em></p> <p>One request handles the whole loop. The Gateway ARN goes in as an MCP connector, and Bedrock takes it from there; pulling the tool list, picking the right one, invoking it, and feeding the result back to the model without anything leaving AWS. The client never sees credentials, tool schemas, or the intermediate turns. See <a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-server-side-tool-call" target="_blank" rel="noopener">ShopAssist: E-Commerce Agent Demo</a> for more details.</p> <p>Early results: For specialist agents with short, focused tool loops, early measurements show approximately 30% lower latency. A sales agent calling three tools in sequence (check inventory, apply discount, update cart) collapses 50+ lines of orchestration into a single API call. All credentials stay server-side, and the simplified architecture makes onboarding new agent developers faster.</p> <h3 id="integration-ecosystem-expansion-ongoing">Integration ecosystem expansion (ongoing)</h3> <p>AgentFlo adds integrations based on merchant demand. Each new platform (payment processors, shipping providers, loyalty systems, or vertical-specific ERPs) becomes another MCP server connector in AgentCore Gateway. This pattern keeps expansion modular and quick.</p> <h2 id="key-takeaways">Key takeaways</h2> <ol type="1"> <li><strong>Pick your pillars first. The right AWS stack follows.</strong> AgentFlo first defined what velocity, standardization, scalability, and trust meant for the production system. With clear requirements, the AWS stack choices became obvious: Strands for the agent layer, AgentCore runtime for stateful sessions, AgentCore Gateway for tool routing and policy, and AWS Fargate for message ingestion.</li> <li><strong>Specialized agent recipes beat multi-agent complexity.</strong> Deploying a single, well-configured agent with domain-specific tools and knowledge outperforms multi-agent orchestration for most customer interactions. Multi-agent handoffs are reserved for cross-domain transitions where context boundaries are clearly defined.</li> <li><strong>Commerce is stateful. Plan for that on day one.</strong> A real sales journey can span 8 hours or 3 days. Stateless chatbots can’t maintain context that long. AgentCore runtime stateful sessions and DynamoDB-backed context were chosen on day one for exactly this reason. Retrofitting state onto a stateless agent later is much more painful than designing for it up front.</li> <li><strong>Three-layer security builds trust.</strong> Pre-turn guards on Fargate, per-tool guards through AgentCore Gateway policies with Cedar, and post-turn output filters work together to support safe deployment of autonomous agents handling commercial transactions.</li> <li><strong>System design supports scale.</strong> By building agent customization as a software as a service (SaaS) layer on top of AgentCore infrastructure, AgentFlo serves hundreds of merchants on shared infrastructure while still delivering personalized agent behavior for each one.</li> <li><strong>Serverless + AgentCore = elastic commerce.</strong> The combination of Fargate for message handling and AgentCore for agent execution means AgentFlo scales from normal traffic to 50x flash-sale spikes without pre-provisioning or capacity planning.</li> <li><strong>The feedback loop is the product.</strong> Real customer conversations teach the agents how to close. Where customers hesitate, what language converts, when they want a human handoff — all of it feeds back into recipes, prompts, and tool definitions. A new merchant deployment benefits from every conversation that ran on the platform before it. That compounding loop is harder to copy than any single piece of the architecture.</li> </ol> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>Building production-scale AI agents for commerce requires scalability and trust from day one. By combining Amazon Bedrock AgentCore stateful sessions and microVM isolation with AWS serverless infrastructure, AgentFlo delivers autonomous agents that handle unpredictable traffic while maintaining the security controls enterprises require.</p> <h3 id="next-steps">Next steps</h3> <ul> <li><a href="https://github.com/strands-agents/sdk-python" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Get started with the Strands Agents SDK</a>.</li> <li><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Explore Amazon Bedrock AgentCore capabilities</a>.</li> <li><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Review Amazon Bedrock pricing</a>.</li> <li><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/how-agentflo-built-ai-sales-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-part-1/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Read Part 1 for velocity and standardization patterns</a>.</li> </ul> <p>For questions about implementing similar architectures, visit the <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/architecture/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Architecture Center</a> or contact your AWS account team. To start building, open the <a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock console</a> or explore the <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agents/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock service detail page</a>.</p> <p>We’d love to hear how you’re building agentic AI systems. Share your experiences in the comments.</p> <hr> <h2>About the authors</h2>

Build a unified AI agent architecture with DynamoDB and Bedrock

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/build-a-unified-ai-agent-architecture-with-dynamodb-and-bedrock/📅 2026-08-21 18:19 UTC

<p>Teams building AI agents on AWS often face a fragmented data architecture: operational data lives in <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon DynamoDB</a> while vector embeddings for semantic search sit in a separate, purpose-built vector store. This duplication increases infrastructure cost, adds synchronization complexity, and widens the window for stale retrieval results. With the general availability of native vector search in Amazon DynamoDB (<a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/amazon-dynamodb-vector-search/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">launched August 5, 2026</a>), you can now store embeddings alongside your operational data in the same table. You query them using the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/APIReference/API_SearchVectors.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">SearchVectors</a> API operation.</p> <p>In this post, I show you how to build a unified AI agent architecture where an <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock</a> agent uses a single DynamoDB table for both structured lookups and semantic similarity search. The agent calls <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Lambda</a> action groups that invoke <code>SearchVectors</code> for natural language retrieval and standard DynamoDB APIs for create, read, update, and delete (CRUD) operations. An <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/Streams.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon DynamoDB Streams</a> pipeline automatically generates embeddings using Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2 whenever content changes. This keeps the vector index synchronized without manual intervention.</p> <h2 id="use-case">Use case</h2> <p>Consider a technical knowledge management platform where a team maintains hundreds of internal documents: runbooks, architecture decision records, and troubleshooting guides. Team members interact with a conversational agent to find relevant content (“What’s our retry strategy for payment failures?”), retrieve specific documents by ID, or update existing entries.</p> <p>Without native vector search, this architecture requires a DynamoDB table for document storage plus a separate vector database (or <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon OpenSearch Service</a> cluster) for semantic retrieval. The <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/Streams.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon DynamoDB Streams</a> pipeline must write to both stores, and the agent must route requests to the correct backend. With DynamoDB vector search, you collapse this into a single table and reduce operational overhead.</p> <h2 id="solution-overview">Solution overview</h2> <p>This solution uses a single-table design in DynamoDB that serves two access patterns: key-value lookups for operational data and approximate nearest neighbor (ANN) search for semantic queries. A Bedrock agent orchestrates user interactions and routes requests to the appropriate action group function.</p> <p>The following list summarizes the core components:</p> <ul> <li>DynamoDB table with vector index stores documents, metadata, and 1,024-dimension embeddings in one place.</li> <li>Bedrock agent handles conversation orchestration, tool selection, and response synthesis.</li> <li>Action group Lambda executes semantic search (using <code>SearchVectors</code>) and CRUD operations against the same table.</li> <li>Embedding pipeline Lambda (triggered by DynamoDB Streams) generates embeddings for new or modified content using <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/titan-embedding-models.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2</a>.</li> </ul> <h2 id="architecture">Architecture</h2> <p>The following diagram illustrates the data flow through the unified architecture.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/08/21/ARCHBLOG-1687-1.jpg" alt="Architecture diagram showing a user query flowing to an Amazon Bedrock agent, which invokes action group Lambda functions that call the DynamoDB SearchVectors API and standard CRUD APIs, with DynamoDB Streams triggering an embedding pipeline Lambda that generates vectors with Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2" width="800"> <p class="wp-caption-text"> <br> Figure 1: Unified AI agent architecture using DynamoDB vector search and Amazon Bedrock </p> </div> <p>The numbered steps describe the data and request flow:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>A user sends a natural language query to the Bedrock agent.</li> <li>The agent analyzes the request and invokes the appropriate action group Lambda function.</li> <li>For semantic search, the action group Lambda generates a query embedding using Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2.</li> <li>The Lambda function calls the DynamoDB <code>SearchVectors</code> API (or standard CRUD APIs for operational lookups) against the single table with vector index.</li> <li>When new content is written to the table, DynamoDB Streams captures the change.</li> <li>DynamoDB Streams triggers the embedding pipeline Lambda.</li> <li>The embedding pipeline Lambda calls Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2 to generate a vector for the new content and writes it back to the same DynamoDB item, where the vector index automatically indexes it.</li> </ol> <h2 id="prerequisites">Prerequisites</h2> <p>To implement this architecture in your account, you need the following:</p> <ul> <li>An AWS account with permissions to create DynamoDB tables, Lambda functions, Bedrock agents, and IAM roles.</li> <li>DynamoDB Streams enabled on the table with <code>StreamViewType</code> set to <code>NEW_AND_OLD_IMAGES</code> (the embedding pipeline compares old and new content to prevent a write loop).</li> <li>Access to the Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2 model (<code>amazon.titan-embed-text-v2:0</code>) enabled in <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-access.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock model access</a>.</li> <li>Access to an Anthropic Claude or Amazon Nova model for the Bedrock agent foundation model (check <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">model support by Region</a>).</li> <li>Python 3.12 or later (for Lambda function code).</li> </ul> <h2 id="implementation">Implementation</h2> <p>This section walks through the key components of the architecture.</p> <h3 id="designing-the-single-table-schema">Designing the single-table schema</h3> <p>The table uses a composite primary key (<code>entity_id</code> as partition key, <code>sk</code> as sort key) and stores embeddings as a list of numbers:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-plaintext"># Table schema overview # PK: entity_id (S) - unique document identifier # SK: sk (S) - sort key for item versioning # Attributes: title, content, category, metadata, embedding (L of N)</code></pre> </div> <p>The vector index partitions search results by the <code>category</code> attribute. Choose a partition key with moderate cardinality that matches your query patterns. A very low-cardinality key (a handful of values) concentrates data in few partitions and limits throughput scaling, while a unique-per-item key leaves no neighbors to compare. For multi-tenant workloads, <code>tenant_id</code> is usually the right partition key. For more information, refer to the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/VectorSearch.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">DynamoDB vector search best practices</a>.</p> <p>The following AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) command creates the vector index on an existing table:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">aws dynamodb update-table \ --table-name unified-agent-data \ --stream-specification StreamEnabled=true,StreamViewType=NEW_AND_OLD_IMAGES \ --attribute-definitions \ AttributeName=category,AttributeType=S \ --vector-index-updates \ '[{"Create": { "IndexName": "content-embedding-index", "VectorAttribute": {"AttributeName": "embedding"}, "Dimensions": 1024, "DistanceFunction": "COSINE", "SearchSchema": [ {"AttributeName": "category", "SearchSchemaElementType": "HASH"} ], "Projection": {"ProjectionType": "INCLUDE", "NonKeyAttributes": ["title", "category"]} }}]'</code></pre> </div> <p>After creating the index, wait for it to become searchable. Poll <code>DescribeTable</code> until <code>IndexStatus</code> is <code>ACTIVE</code> and <code>Backfilling</code> is no longer <code>true</code>. The first few searches after the index reports <code>ACTIVE</code> can still return <code>ValidationException</code> because <code>SearchVectors</code> is served by a dedicated search endpoint. Treat these as retryable rather than as a failure.</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">aws dynamodb describe-table --table-name unified-agent-data \ --query 'Table.VectorIndexes[?IndexName==`content-embedding-index`].[IndexStatus,Backfilling]'</code></pre> </div> <p>Key constraints to keep in mind:</p> <ul> <li>DynamoDB vector indexes require on-demand capacity mode (provisioned mode isn’t supported).</li> <li>Maximum five vector indexes per table, with up to 4,096 dimensions each.</li> <li>The <code>SearchSchema</code> HASH attribute is mandatory in every <code>SearchConditionExpression</code>.</li> <li>Only equality operators are supported in search conditions.</li> <li><code>SearchVectors</code> responses are limited to 16 MB and don’t support pagination. Project only the attributes you need and keep <code>TopK</code> modest to stay within this limit.</li> <li>Items missing the <code>SearchSchema</code> HASH attribute (<code>category</code> in this example) are silently excluded from the vector index while remaining in the base table.</li> </ul> <h3 id="building-the-action-group-lambda">Building the action group Lambda</h3> <p>The action group Lambda handles both semantic search and operational lookups. The agent invokes it with a function name and parameters based on the tool definition.</p> <p>The semantic search function generates a query embedding and calls <code>SearchVectors</code>. This index uses <code>COSINE</code> distance, where lower scores indicate greater similarity. Name the field accordingly so the agent doesn’t invert the ranking:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-python">def semantic_search(query: str, category: str, max_results: int = 5): embedding = generate_embedding(query) results = dynamodb.search_vectors( TableName=TABLE_NAME, IndexName=INDEX_NAME, SearchVector=[{"N": str(v)} for v in embedding], TopK=min(max_results, 100), SearchConditionExpression="category = :cat", ExpressionAttributeValues={":cat": {"S": category}}, ) return [ {"entity_id": r["Item"]["entity_id"]["S"], "title": r["Item"].get("title", {}).get("S", ""), "distance": r["Score"]} # COSINE: lower = more similar for r in results.get("SearchResults", []) ]</code></pre> </div> <p>The <code>generate_embedding</code> helper calls Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-python">def generate_embedding(text: str) -&gt; list[float]: response = bedrock_runtime.invoke_model( modelId="amazon.titan-embed-text-v2:0", body=json.dumps({ "inputText": text, "dimensions": 1024, "normalize": True }), ) return json.loads(response["body"].read())["embedding"]</code></pre> </div> <p>The Lambda handler routes requests based on the function name passed by the Bedrock agent:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-python">def handler(event, context): function = event.get("function") parameters = {p["name"]: p["value"] for p in event.get("parameters", [])} if function == "semantic_search": result = semantic_search(parameters["query"], parameters["category"]) body = json.dumps({"results": result}) elif function == "get_item_details": body = json.dumps(get_item_details(parameters["entity_id"])) else: body = json.dumps({"error": f"Unknown function: {function}"}) return { "messageVersion": "1.0", "response": { "actionGroup": event["actionGroup"], "function": function, "functionResponse": {"responseBody": {"TEXT": {"body": body}}} } }</code></pre> </div> <h3 id="automating-embeddings-with-dynamodb-streams">Automating embeddings with DynamoDB Streams</h3> <p>The embedding pipeline Lambda triggers on INSERT and MODIFY events. It generates an embedding for new or changed content and writes it back to the same item:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-python">def handler(event, context): for record in event["Records"]: if record["eventName"] not in ("INSERT", "MODIFY"): continue new_image = record["dynamodb"]["NewImage"] old_image = record["dynamodb"].get("OldImage", {}) content = new_image.get("content", {}).get("S") if not content: continue # Prevent infinite loop: skip if content hasn't changed if "embedding" in new_image and old_image.get("content") == new_image.get("content"): continue embedding = generate_embedding(content) dynamodb.update_item( TableName=TABLE_NAME, Key={"entity_id": new_image["entity_id"], "sk": new_image["sk"]}, UpdateExpression="SET embedding = :emb", ExpressionAttributeValues={ ":emb": {"L": [{"N": str(v)} for v in embedding]} }, )</code></pre> </div> <p>The infinite-loop guard is critical. Without it, the Lambda writes back an embedding, which triggers another Streams event, which triggers another embedding generation, and so on. The check compares the content field between old and new images, skipping processing when only the embedding attribute changed. This guard requires <code>StreamViewType = NEW_AND_OLD_IMAGES</code>. Without it, <code>OldImage</code> is empty and the guard never fires.</p> <p>For production use, configure the event source mapping with <code>ReportBatchItemFailures</code> so that only failed records are retried. Add an Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS) dead-letter queue (or on-failure destination) for records that repeatedly fail. Retry Amazon Bedrock <code>InvokeModel</code> calls with exponential backoff to handle throttling.</p> <h3 id="defining-the-agent-tool-schema">Defining the agent tool schema</h3> <p>The Bedrock agent needs a function schema that describes the available tools. This tells the agent when and how to call each function:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-json">{ "functions": [ { "name": "semantic_search", "description": "Search documents by meaning using natural language. Returns results ranked by COSINE distance (lower = more similar).", "parameters": { "query": {"type": "string", "required": true, "description": "Natural language search query"}, "category": {"type": "string", "required": true, "description": "Document category to search within"} } }, { "name": "get_item_details", "description": "Retrieve a specific document by its unique ID.", "parameters": { "entity_id": {"type": "string", "required": true, "description": "Unique document identifier"} } } ] }</code></pre> </div> <h3 id="when-to-use-this-pattern">When to use this pattern</h3> <p>This unified architecture works best when your application already uses DynamoDB as its primary operational store and you want to add semantic search without managing a separate service. Consider the following decision points:</p> <ul> <li>Use this pattern when your application meets these conditions: <ul> <li>Documents update frequently and must be immediately searchable.</li> <li>Your dataset fits within the DynamoDB vector index constraints.</li> <li>You want to minimize infrastructure components.</li> </ul></li> <li>Use Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases when your source data lives in Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3), you need managed chunking and ingestion, or you don’t need real-time index updates tied to operational writes.</li> <li>Use Amazon OpenSearch Service when you need advanced search features (range filters, aggregations, faceted search), your queries require more than equality-based filtering, or you need results beyond the 100-item <code>TopK</code> limit.</li> </ul> <h2 id="security-considerations">Security considerations</h2> <p>The following list highlights the key security aspects of this architecture:</p> <ul> <li>Least-privilege IAM policies: Scope <code>dynamodb:SearchVectors</code> to the specific index ARN (<code>arn:aws:dynamodb:{region}:{account}:table/{table}/index/{index}</code>). The embedding Lambda needs only <code>dynamodb:UpdateItem</code>, not search permissions.</li> <li>No fine-grained access control for SearchVectors: DynamoDB condition keys like <code>dynamodb:LeadingKeys</code> don’t apply to the <code>SearchVectors</code> API. For multi-tenant workloads, use the <code>SearchSchema</code> HASH partition key to scope queries by tenant, or use separate tables for strict isolation.</li> <li>Encryption at rest: DynamoDB encrypts data including vector embeddings using your choice of AWS owned keys, AWS managed keys, or customer managed keys through <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/kms/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS)</a>.</li> <li>Transport encryption: All <code>SearchVectors</code> traffic uses TLS. The API routes to a dedicated search endpoint that the AWS SDKs handle automatically.</li> <li>Bedrock model access: Restrict <code>bedrock:InvokeModel</code> permissions to the specific embedding and agent foundation model ARNs required by the solution.</li> <li>Agent-to-Lambda invocation: Grant <code>lambda:InvokeFunction</code> to <code>bedrock.amazonaws.com</code> on the action group Lambda, scoped with an <code>aws:SourceArn</code> condition matching the agent ARN. Without this resource-based policy, the agent can’t invoke the action group.</li> </ul> <h2 id="clean-up">Clean up</h2> <p>To avoid ongoing charges, delete the resources in the following order:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>Delete the Bedrock agent and its action group.</li> <li>Delete the embedding pipeline Lambda function and its event source mapping.</li> <li>Delete the DynamoDB table (this also removes the vector index). If you want to keep the table but remove the vector index, run the following command first: <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">aws dynamodb update-table \ --table-name unified-agent-data \ --vector-index-updates '[{"Delete": {"IndexName": "content-embedding-index"}}]'</code></pre> </div></li> <li>Delete the action group Lambda function and associated IAM roles.</li> </ol> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>With this pattern, you can build a unified AI agent architecture that uses a single DynamoDB table for both operational data and vector-based semantic search. The native vector search of DynamoDB combined with Bedrock agent action groups eliminates the need for a separate vector database. DynamoDB Streams-driven embedding generation keeps the index synchronized in real time.</p> <p>This pattern reduces infrastructure complexity for applications that already rely on DynamoDB and need to add conversational AI capabilities. The automatic embedding pipeline keeps your vector index synchronized with operational writes, and the action group design gives the agent access to both semantic and structured query paths.</p> <p>Adapt the table schema, embedding dimensions, and agent instructions to your domain. Clone the <a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-dynamodb-vector-search-architecture" target="_blank" rel="noopener">sample-dynamodb-vector-search-architecture</a> repository to deploy the complete working implementation. For more information about DynamoDB vector search capabilities and limits, refer to the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/VectorSearch.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon DynamoDB vector search documentation</a>.</p> <h2 id="references">References</h2> <ul> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/VectorSearch.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon DynamoDB vector search documentation</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/agents.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock Agents documentation</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/titan-embedding-models.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2</a></li> </ul> <h2>About the author</h2>

How a global payment processor preserved AWS RAM shares and Lake Formation permissions during an AWS Organizations migration

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/how-a-global-payment-processor-preserved-aws-ram-shares-and-lake-formation-permissions-during-an-aws-organizations-migration/📅 2026-08-24 15:23 UTC

<p>Accounts move between organizations in <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/organizations/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Organizations</a> whenever a business changes shape. A merger folds one estate into another. A divestiture carves one out, and some companies run more than one organization by design.</p> <p>The moves take more care when <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ram/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Resource Access Manager</a> (AWS RAM) resource shares are involved. An organization-bound share trusts an account through its organization membership, so when the account leaves, AWS RAM removes that association. Anything in production that depends on a shared resource needs a continuity plan before the first account moves.</p> <p>A leading worldwide provider of payment technology and software solutions, based out of the United States, used temporary AWS RAM resource shares to preserve <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lake-formation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Lake Formation permissions</a> during an <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/organizations/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Organizations</a> migration of 382 AWS accounts. The payment processor serves merchants and financial institutions around the world. The program separated them from their former parent company, a US-headquartered financial technology provider serving banking and capital markets clients globally, before a Transitional Service Agreement (TSA) expired in April 2026.</p> <p>The migration unfolded alongside wider corporate change. In January 2026, the payment processor was acquired by a leading payments technology company headquartered in the United States. The transaction transforms the acquirer into a pure-play commerce solutions provider, serving the full spectrum of clients from small businesses to global enterprises worldwide. The migrating estate also included the payment processor’s embedded payments platform, a US-based provider of embedded payment and automated onboarding tools for software-as-a-service (SaaS) platforms.</p> <p>Most workloads kept running when the original organization-bound shares broke, but the control plane lost access. They needed a migration pattern that preserved service continuity without leaving temporary permissions behind.</p> <p>AWS partnered with them to design and validate that pattern in two weeks. It uses retained bridge shares for the move, then restores the original shares as the durable permission objects.</p> <p>In this post, we explain why the bridge works, why the original share must return, and how the company applied the pattern at enterprise scale. For command-level implementation, see <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/mt/transfer-aws-accounts-between-aws-organizations-while-preserving-aws-lake-formation-permissions/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Transfer AWS accounts between AWS Organizations while preserving AWS Lake Formation permissions</a> and <a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-aws-ram-org-migration" target="_blank" rel="noopener">aws-samples/sample-aws-ram-org-migration</a>.</p> <h2 id="solution-overview">Solution overview</h2> <p>An AWS account can consume a resource to share automatically while it belongs to the same organization as the producer. When the account leaves, AWS RAM removes that organization-bound principal association. Creating a retained bridge share as an external association before the move preserves access through the organization’s change.</p> <p>After the move, the company restores the migrated account to the original share, verifies access, and removes the bridge. The original share remains the <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lake-formation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Lake Formation</a>-managed source of truth. New grants and resource changes continue to attach to it, not to the point-in-time bridge copy. Keeping both would create duplicate permission state and drift.</p> <p>The following diagram shows the migration wave structure.</p> <figure> <img title="Bridge share pattern" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/08/17/ARCHBLOG-1650-1.png" alt="Migration wave structure. Stage one covers fourteen non-production waves across eight months, none of which crossed an organization boundary. Stage two covers sixteen production waves: one pilot wave, twelve scheduled waves on a weekly cadence, and three contingency waves. The transitional service agreement expires inside the contingency window, leaving only the first contingency week usable." width="800"> <figcaption aria-hidden="true">Migration wave structure. Stage one covers fourteen non-production waves across eight months, none of which crossed an organization boundary. Stage two covers sixteen production waves: one pilot wave, twelve scheduled waves on a weekly cadence, and three contingency waves. The transitional service agreement expires inside the contingency window, leaving only the first contingency week usable.</figcaption> </figure> <p>The company’s cloud engineering team ran 14 non-production waves, a production pilot, 12 weekly production waves, and three contingency waves. The TSA expired inside the contingency window, leaving about one week of usable slack.</p> <h2 id="challenge">Challenge</h2> <p>The risk surfaced in a production wave in February 2026. A <code>terraform apply</code> against a shared <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/transit-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Transit Gateway</a> failed with a permission error, although traffic kept flowing and no alarm fired.</p> <p>This was a control plane failure. Existing attachments, DNS paths, and certificates continued to work, but engineers could not change shared resources. The dedicated <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/transit-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Transit Gateway</a>, <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/route53/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Route 53 Resolver</a>, and the embedded payments platform’s <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/glue/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Glue Data Catalog</a> waves were still ahead.</p> <h3 id="why-the-issue-stayed-hidden">Why the issue stayed hidden</h3> <p>Most services retain their data plane when an AWS RAM association breaks. For example, an <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud</a> (Amazon EC2) instance in a shared <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/vpc/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Virtual Private Cloud</a> (Amazon VPC) keeps running, but the account cannot launch a new instance. Infrastructure-as-code exposed the problem because it needed control-plane access.</p> <p>The following table summarizes the affected resource types confirmed by the customer.</p> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Resource</strong></td> <td><strong>AWS service</strong></td> <td><strong>Effect of losing the share</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>AWS Transit Gateway</td> <td><code>ec2:TransitGateway</code></td> <td>Loses control plane access, keeps the data plane</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Amazon Route 53 Resolver rules</td> <td><code>route53resolver:ResolverRule</code></td> <td>Risk of DNS resolution disruption</td> </tr> <tr> <td><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/private-ca/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Private Certificate Authority</a> (AWS Private CA)</td> <td><code>acm-pca:CertificateAuthority</code></td> <td>Loses the share, issued certificates keep working</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Amazon EC2 prefix lists</td> <td><code>ec2:PrefixList</code></td> <td>Keeps the data plane, blocks new resource creation</td> </tr> <tr> <td>AWS Glue Data Catalog databases and tables</td> <td>AWS Glue and AWS Lake Formation</td> <td>Requires bridge-share validation before migration</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <p>Some services require additional handling. Depending on its resource-cleanup configuration, an <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/firewall-manager/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Firewall Manager</a> policy can remove <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/network-firewall/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Network Firewall</a> rules, which you must then redeploy, and organization-integrated <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudformation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS CloudFormation</a> StackSets can delete stacks unless you set them to retain.</p> <p>The embedded payments platform initially entered the estate through an acquisition by the former parent company. Its embedded payment and automated onboarding capabilities were subsequently integrated into the payment processor’s ecosystem to support a broader platform-focused offering for SaaS providers. The platform shared databases and tables across 10 accounts with account IDs as principals, and the workstream was paused rather than testing the migration against production.</p> <h3 id="why-the-original-shares-broke">Why the original shares broke</h3> <p>The company had enabled sharing with AWS Organizations in the producer account. AWS RAM therefore trusted each in-organization principal through organization membership, even when a share named an account ID. When an account left the source organization, AWS RAM removed that organization-bound association.</p> <p>A share created for a principal outside the organization behaves differently. AWS RAM sends an invitation, and the accepted association is external. Because it does not depend on organization membership, it survives the account move. The bridge-share pattern uses this behavior.</p> <h3 id="why-non-production-testing-missed-it">Why non-production testing missed it</h3> <p>The company’s non-production accounts were already in a separate organization. They never crossed the boundary that caused production associations to break.</p> <p>Fourteen clean waves validated the migration process but not the production-only condition. Each validation environment must cross the same trust boundary as production.</p> <h2 id="applying-the-bridge-share-pattern">Applying the bridge-share pattern</h2> <p>This failure was raised with the AWS account team, which brought AWS RAM, AWS Glue, AWS Lake Formation, and AWS Organizations service teams into the response. The company first used a manual recovery path for 21 accounts while the teams automated a scalable approach.</p> <p>On February 27, 2026, AWS released <code>RetainSharingOnAccountLeaveOrganization</code> for new AWS RAM resource shares. The setting marks principals as external after they accept the invitation. The customer confirmed that the setting does not retrofit existing shares, so those shares needed a temporary parallel share.</p> <h3 id="retaining-access-during-the-move">Retaining access during the move</h3> <p>AWS RAM allows a resource to belong to more than one resource share, so a parallel share can exist alongside the original. A second retained share was created alongside each original, targeting the same consumer account.</p> <p>The consumer accepted the invitation before migration, creating an external association. During the move, AWS RAM removed the original organization-bound association while the bridge continued to grant access. AWS Organizations also support transferring an account directly between organizations, so the move itself does not require an intermediate standalone period.</p> <h3 id="why-restore-the-original-share">Why restore the original share?</h3> <p>The bridge is a migration-only continuity copy. The original AWS Lake Formation-created share remains the durable, service-managed permission object. If a team adds a grant or changes a shared resource during the migration window, that change applies to the original share, not automatically to the bridge.</p> <p>The company therefore restored migrated principals to the original share before deleting the bridge. Leaving both in place would create two permission paths that can diverge, complicate audits, and conceal which share is authoritative.</p> <p>Automation deletes a bridge only after it finds a non-bridge original whose resources, principals, and permissions cover the bridge and whose associations are all <code>ASSOCIATED</code>. This check confirms the original share’s associations are active before removing the temporary path. Access and connectivity were validated separately, as described in the Outcome section.</p> <h3 id="validated-workflow">Validated workflow</h3> <p>AWS validated the pattern across three test accounts and two organizations before it was used in production. Testing confirmed that <code>allowExternalPrincipals</code> alone was not enough. The bridge also required <code>retainSharingOnAccountLeaveOrganization</code>.</p> <p>The following diagram shows the bridge before and after the account move.</p> <figure> <img title="Bridge share pattern" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/08/21/arch-1650-3.png" alt="Bridge share behavior before and after an account moves between AWS Organizations. Before the move, the original organization-scoped share and the accepted bridge share both grant access. After the move, the original share is revoked and the accepted bridge share continues to grant access." width="800"> <figcaption aria-hidden="true">Bridge share behavior before and after an account moves between AWS Organizations. Before the move, the original organization-scoped share and the accepted bridge share both grant access. After the move, the original share is revoked and the accepted bridge share continues to grant access.</figcaption> </figure> <p>Each production wave used five steps:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>Inventory. Map each original share, resource, principal, permission, and Region. AWS RAM is Regional, so repeat the inventory in every in-scope Region.</li> <li>Create and accept bridges. Create a retained share for the same resource and principals, then accept its invitation from each consumer account before migration.</li> <li>Migrate. Move the account. AWS RAM removes the organization-bound association, while the accepted bridge keeps access active.</li> <li>Restore originals. Add the migrated account IDs back to the original shares as external principals. This reactivates the durable shares and includes grants created during the migration window.</li> <li>Validate and remove bridges. Confirm resources, principals, permissions, and association status, then delete only bridge shares fully covered by active originals.</li> </ol> <p>This workflow ran for every remaining production wave and kept the weekly cadence.</p> <h3 id="validating-aws-glue-and-lake-formation-permissions">Validating AWS Glue and Lake Formation permissions</h3> <p>The embedded payments platform shared AWS Glue Data Catalog databases and tables across 10 accounts, with account IDs as principals. The configuration was reproduced in disposable accounts, and the resource policy was recorded through a cross-organization move.</p> <p>The validated automation records principal-to-share mappings, supports dry-run and execute modes, restores principals to the original shares, and deletes bridges only after validation. The embedded payments platform completed its production migration on July 21, 2026.</p> <h2 id="outcome">Outcome</h2> <ul> <li>The global payment processor migrated 378 of the 382 accounts into its landing zone. The final four awaited approvals from external stakeholders.</li> </ul> <p>The TSA with the former parent company ended on schedule in April 2026. No customer-facing workload lost availability, and the company recorded no network drops across the production waves. The company and AWS moved from discovery to a validated bridge-share pattern in two weeks.</p> <p>After each migration, the cloud engineering team restored the original shares, verified access and connectivity, and removed the bridges. Deleting the temporary copies confirmed that the original service-managed permission path was active and authoritative.</p> <p>Production, non-production, and the embedded payments platform now run in one landing zone. They control their own guardrails, security posture, provisioning, and change process.</p> <p>AWS has published the validated pattern and automation, so other organizations can start with a tested procedure.</p> <h2 id="lessons-learned">Lessons learned</h2> <p>This program produced three lessons for organizations planning similar migrations.</p> <h3 id="match-validation-boundaries-to-production">Match validation boundaries to production</h3> <p>A test organization cannot expose this failure unless it crosses the same organization boundary as production. Map each production risk to an environment that can reproduce it before the first wave.</p> <h3 id="monitor-control-plane-changes">Monitor control plane changes</h3> <p>AWS RAM emits resource share state-change events directly to <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eventbridge/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon EventBridge</a>, and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudtrail/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS CloudTrail</a> records <code>DisassociateResourceShare</code> API calls for audit. A weekly post-migration sweep provided a periodic reconciliation check to catch stale shares.</p> <h3 id="inventory-dependencies-and-destination-guardrails">Inventory dependencies and destination guardrails</h3> <p>The <a href="https://github.com/aws-solutions/account-assessment-for-aws-organizations" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Account Assessment for AWS Organizations</a> tool inventories AWS RAM dependencies before teams set the wave plan. The company’s cloud engineering team reviewed destination guardrails at the same time. A service control policy that blocked <code>ram:AcceptResourceShareInvitation</code> during migration windows was temporarily adjusted.</p> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>The migration of this leading payment technology and software company shows how a retained bridge share can protect access while an account moves between AWS Organizations. The bridge is temporary: restoring the original share keeps AWS Lake Formation permissions aligned with future grants and avoids two sources of permission state. Inventory, dry-run-first automation, and post-move validation helped the company meet its deadline without customer disruption.</p> <h2 id="next-steps">Next steps</h2> <p>To apply this pattern, read <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/mt/transfer-aws-accounts-between-aws-organizations-while-preserving-aws-lake-formation-permissions/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Transfer AWS accounts between AWS Organizations while preserving AWS Lake Formation permissions</a> and review <a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-aws-ram-org-migration" target="_blank" rel="noopener">aws-samples/sample-aws-ram-org-migration</a>. Run the scripts in dry-run mode, validate each Region and account, and engage your AWS account team early when AWS Glue Data Catalog or AWS Lake Formation resources are in scope.</p> <hr> <h2>About the authors</h2>
