Closing the AI agent trust gap with graduated autonomy

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/closing-the-ai-agent-trust-gap-with-graduated-autonomy/📅 2026-08-26 17:33 UTC

<p>How much to trust an AI agent is now a daily operational question. Agents read customer data, open tickets, process refunds, and delete accounts, yet most teams pick up a binary: full access or read-only. Full access is risky because agents fail unpredictably. Read-only leaves most of the agent’s value unused. The distance between what an agent could do and what an operator trusts it to do is the agent’s trust gap.</p> <p>In this post, we describe graduated autonomy, an architectural pattern that closes the gap. Agents earn expanded permissions through sustained reliability and lose them when performance degrades. <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</a>, a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale with any framework or model, provides the runtime, gateway, policy, and evaluation capabilities. <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon DynamoDB</a> stores trust state. <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/codepipeline/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS CodePipeline</a> gates delivery on evaluation results. We cover each layer’s responsibility and the key design decision behind it.</p> <h2 id="the-agent-trust-gap">The agent trust gap</h2> <p>Identity and access management answers “who can do what?” once, at provisioning. That model assumes that the principal behaves consistently. A large language model agent breaks it: the same agent can be accurate Monday and hallucinated Tuesday after a prompt change or model update.</p> <p>Closing the gap requires three capabilities raw API logs rarely provide:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Visibility.</strong> API logs tell engineers what happened but tell a compliance officer nothing about whether an action was safe.</li> <li><strong>Decision provenance.</strong> Tracing an action back to the signal that triggered it, the alternatives considered, and the confidence held.</li> <li><strong>Reversibility.</strong> Pre-action state capture, so operators can recover from incorrect actions.</li> </ul> <p>The framework that implements this pattern delivers all three through six architectural layers.</p> <h2 id="solution-overview">Solution overview</h2> <p>The six layers:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Scoring engine</strong> computes trust from configurable dimensions.</li> <li><strong>Tier system</strong> translates sustained scores into autonomy levels.</li> <li><strong>Pre-execution layer</strong> blocks dangerous actions before they run.</li> <li><strong>Enforcement layer</strong> applies tiers through Cedar policies at the infrastructure level.</li> <li><strong>Post-execution layer</strong> evaluates outcomes, records of provenance, and feeds signals back to scoring.</li> <li><strong>Delivery gate</strong> keeps degraded agent versions out of production.</li> </ul> <p></p> <div style="width: 666px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/08/25/ARCHBLOG-1524-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram of the trust framework as a clockwise closed loop: the scoring engine produces a weighted trust score from five dimensions, the tier system converts sustained scores into autonomy tiers T1 through T4, the pre-execution and enforcement layers apply the current tier through in-process checks and Cedar policies, and the post-execution layer returns outcome scores, honeypot results, and human overrides to the scoring engine, with an audit trail at the center recording every decision." width="656"> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 1: The trust framework’s closed loop.</p> </div> <br> Each layer is replaceable: the scoring model, tier thresholds, pre-execution signals, and evaluation criteria are configuration, not code. Each layer also embodies one deliberate design decision, developed in the following sections: <p></p> <table border="1px" cellpadding="10px" width="100%"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Layer</strong></td> <td><strong>Key design decision</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Scoring engine</td> <td>Safety is an independent floor, never averaged away by strong metrics</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Tier system</td> <td>Start every agent at T1. Promote slowly, demote immediately</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Pre-execution layer</td> <td>Fast in-process filters are backstopped, never solely trusted</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Enforcement layer</td> <td>Deny by default, enforced outside the agent’s process</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Post-execution layer</td> <td>Audit records capture pre-action state, making recovery possible</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Delivery gate</td> <td>One unauthorized tool call in adversarial tests blocks release</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <h2 id="the-scoring-engine">The scoring engine</h2> <p>The scoring engine computes a weighted score from 0 to 100 per agent over a rolling window of 50 actions, from five dimensions:</p> <table border="1px" cellpadding="10px" width="100%"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Dimension</strong></td> <td><strong>Weight</strong></td> <td><strong>What it measures</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Accuracy</td> <td>25%</td> <td>Task completion correctness against expected outcomes</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Safety</td> <td>20%</td> <td>Boundary respect, adversarial content detection, permitted tool adherence</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Consistency</td> <td>20%</td> <td>Behavioral predictability, inverse of tool-use pattern drift</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Compliance</td> <td>20%</td> <td>Reasoning quality before acting, guardrail adherence</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Efficiency</td> <td>15%</td> <td>Execution without unnecessary retries or resource waste</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <p>The composite drives dashboards and tier assignment, but safety acts as an independent floor, so a dangerous individual metric never hides strength elsewhere.</p> <h2 id="the-tier-system">The tier system</h2> <p>Every new agent starts at T1, regardless of test performance:</p> <table border="1px" cellpadding="10px" width="100%"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Tier</strong></td> <td><strong>Score range</strong></td> <td><strong>Permissions</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>T1: Probation</td> <td>0 to 40</td> <td>Read and list only. Two tools visible.</td> </tr> <tr> <td>T2: Supervised</td> <td>41 to 70</td> <td>Add write operations. Human approves high-risk.</td> </tr> <tr> <td>T3: Trusted</td> <td>71 to 90</td> <td>Execute and modify. Anomalies flagged for review.</td> </tr> <tr> <td>T4: Autonomous</td> <td>91 to 100</td> <td>Full access. Post-hoc audit only.</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <p>Three rules govern transitions:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Promotion requires sustained performance.</strong> The score must stay above the promotion threshold for the entire rolling window.</li> <li><strong>Demotion is immediate.</strong> When safety drops below its floor or injection is detected, the agent moves down.</li> <li><strong>Hysteresis prevents oscillation.</strong> Promotion into a tier requires a score 5 points above that tier range floor. Demotion happens at the range floor itself. An agent at a boundary cannot flap between tiers.</li> </ul> <p>Trust state lives in Amazon DynamoDB as a current state record plus a time-series history per agent. Enforcement components read the current tier on every invocation, a lookup DynamoDB <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/Introduction.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">typically serves in single-digit milliseconds</a>.</p> <h2 id="the-pre-execution-layer">The pre-execution layer</h2> <p>Post-execution evaluation cannot undo damage, so the pre-execution layer evaluates every tool’s call and can block it before execution. It scores six signals independently:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Adversarial injection detection.</strong> Pattern matching against known injection phrases. One match triggers an instant block and a trust penalty.</li> <li><strong>Sensitive target detection.</strong> Regex matching credentials, tokens, and private keys in tool arguments.</li> <li><strong>Dangerous tool detection.</strong> Flagging tools that match destructive operation patterns.</li> <li><strong>Behavioral consistency.</strong> Comparing the current tool call against the agent’s historical tool-use distribution.</li> <li><strong>Confidence calibration.</strong> Comparing stated confidence against historical accuracy. Overconfident failures are penalized at twice the normal rate.</li> <li><strong>Reasoning quality.</strong> Checking whether the agent provided reasoning before acting.</li> </ul> <p>These checks are fast first-pass filters, not a complete defense. The enforcement layer’s deny-by-default policies backstop anything they miss.</p> <h2 id="the-enforcement-layer">The enforcement layer</h2> <p>The pre-execution layer is application code inside the agent’s process. The enforcement layer operates outside the agent, at the infrastructure level.</p> <p>AgentCore Gateway, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, sits between the agent and its tools. It routes every MCP tool invocation through <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</a>, which evaluates <a href="https://www.cedarpolicy.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Cedar</a> policies with forbid-wins semantics. One satisfied forbid overrides any number of permits. Tier maps to policy state:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Probation:</strong> A forbid policy blocks write, execute, and delete tool actions.</li> <li><strong>Promotion:</strong> The forbid policy is removed, and broader permits take effect.</li> <li><strong>Demotion:</strong> The forbid policy is re-applied.</li> </ul> <p>With the policy engine in enforce mode, <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/use-gateway-with-policy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">the Gateway lists only tools that policy could permit</a>, so the tier’s unconditional forbids keep blocked tools out of the listing. The agent is unlikely to call a tool it has never seen. Listing is a meta-action: each invocation is still evaluated separately with full request context, including input parameters. Cedar denies by default. Enforcement never depends on the agent’s choosing to behave. For model-level content safety, <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/guardrails.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock Guardrails</a> complements Policy in AgentCore, filtering harmful content and masking sensitive information independent of tier.</p> <h2 id="the-post-execution-layer">The post-execution layer</h2> <p>After every tool call, the system scores the outcome across eight signals, from confidence calibration and behavioral drift to human overrides and retry detection. Every action generates an audit record following the Think, Plan, Act, Observe, Score chain:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Think:</strong> The agent’s reasoning chain.</li> <li><strong>Plan:</strong> Tool selected, input prepared, pre-execution score.</li> <li><strong>Act:</strong> Cedar policy matched, Gateway route processed.</li> <li><strong>Observe:</strong> Success or failure, output data.</li> <li><strong>Score:</strong> Trust impact, per-dimension scores, tier change.</li> </ul> <p>The Plan and Act records capture pre-action state, which is what makes recovery from an incorrect action possible. Operators ask questions in plain English, and a provenance query endpoint returns a human-readable explanation of any decision. Audit entries persist to DynamoDB.</p> <h2 id="the-delivery-gate">The delivery gate</h2> <p>Each change to the agent’s prompt, configuration, or tool definitions triggers an AWS CodePipeline run. The run deploys the candidate to staging and runs it against ground-truth fixtures with <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/evaluations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Evaluations</a>, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. The fixtures include adversarial cases such as prompt injection and data-exfiltration requests. A single unauthorized tool call in any adversarial case fails the gate. The version that passes becomes the last known stable version.</p> <h2 id="production-monitoring-and-recovery">Production monitoring and recovery</h2> <p>The framework injects synthetic honeypot cases with known expected behavior into a small share of traffic. Validation checks the tool-call trajectory (expected tools, expected order, no forbidden tools) rather than nondeterministic natural-language output, so a mismatch signals a real anomaly. Honeypot results stay out of production metrics. When safety drops below the floor, demotion narrows the agent’s permissions, and the framework redeploys the last known stable version. Together they restore known-good code alongside a tighter permission set. The framework also alerts operators.</p> <p>Operator judgment feeds directly: the rolling rate at which operators reject proposed actions caps the effective safety metric, so 30 percent rejections cap safety at 70. An emergency stop pushes a single Cedar deny-all policy. Once the policy is active, typically within seconds, the Gateway denies all tool invocations without a redeployment. In multi-agent systems, a delegated action’s effective tier is the minimum across the delegation chain, closing the delegation privilege-escalation path.</p> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>In this post, we described graduated autonomy, an architectural pattern for closing the agent trust gap. With this pattern in place, your agents hold the autonomy their track record supports.</p> <p>To get started, take the dimension weights and tier boundaries from the two tables in this post as a starting template for one agent in your fleet. Start that agent at T1. Then follow the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/evaluations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Evaluations documentation</a> to build the delivery gate, and the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore documentation</a> to write the tier policies. You can explore these capabilities in the <a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock console</a> and on the <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock AgentCore detail page</a>.</p> <p>For deeper dives into the building blocks this pattern uses, read <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/secure-ai-agents-with-policy-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Secure AI agents with Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</a> and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-custom-code-based-evaluators-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Build custom code-based evaluators in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</a>.</p> <hr> <h2>About the authors</h2>

Read full article →

Gallup scales real-time coaching for thousands with Amazon Bedrock

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/gallup-delivers-real-time-workplace-coaching-to-thousands-of-leaders-with-amazon-bedrock/📅 2026-08-26 17:36 UTC

<p>How Gallup turned 90 years of workplace science into an AI assistant that gives leaders personalized guidance in seconds, powered by <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock</a>.</p> <p><a href="https://www.gallup.com/home.aspx" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Gallup</a> delivers analytics and advice to help leaders and organizations solve their most pressing problems. With more than 90 years of experience and a global reach, Gallup has developed a uniquely deep understanding of workplace behavior and performance.</p> <p>However, this knowledge wasn’t centralized or delivered in context. Leaders had to navigate multiple resources to find relevant insights and then translate them into action without guidance. The lack of real-time, personalized recommendations meant workplace challenges were often handled reactively instead of proactively.</p> <p>Gallup needed to transform decades of proprietary research into real-time, personalized guidance that leaders can access instantly within their existing workflow.</p> <p>In this post, we show how Gallup built <a href="https://www.gallup.com/access/697103/gallup-ai.aspx" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Gallup AI</a>, a generative AI assistant powered by Amazon Bedrock. It transforms decades of proprietary workplace research into real-time, personalized coaching delivered directly within the <a href="https://www.gallup.com/access/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Gallup Access</a> application.</p> <h2 id="why-amazon-bedrock">Why Amazon Bedrock</h2> <p>Gallup evaluated multiple approaches to building a generative AI assistant. The team chose Amazon Bedrock for three reasons:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>Access to leading foundation models like <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/anthropic/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Anthropic’s Claude</a> without managing infrastructure.</li> <li>Built-in retrieval augmented generation (RAG) through <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases</a>, a fully managed RAG capability, grounds responses in verified research.</li> <li>Native guardrails to enforce content safety at scale.</li> </ol> <p>This combination allowed Gallup to move from prototype to production in weeks rather than months, without hiring a dedicated machine learning (ML) operations team.</p> <p><em>Note: Anthropic’s Claude models on Amazon Bedrock are available in select AWS Regions. For current model and Region availability, see <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-region-compatibility.html#model-regions-anthropic" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Supported models by Region in Amazon Bedrock.</a></em></p> <h2 id="the-approach-building-intelligence-into-daily-workflow">The approach: Building intelligence into daily workflow</h2> <p>Gallup built <strong>Gallup AI</strong>, a generative AI-powered assistant integrated directly into Gallup Access. The unified application lets managers review engagement results, build action plans, explore <a href="https://www.gallup.com/cliftonstrengths/en/home.aspx" target="_blank" rel="noopener">CliftonStrengths</a> insights, and access curated content to better support their teams.</p> <p>The solution uses Amazon Bedrock with Anthropic’s Claude models to deliver conversational insights grounded in Gallup’s proprietary research. Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/kendra/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Kendra</a> retrieve relevant research and organizational data. This is designed to ground responses in verified workplace science. <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock Guardrails</a> enforce content safety policies, while <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Lambda</a> with FastAPI delivers real-time streaming responses that feel natural and immediate.</p> <p>The architecture follows a <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/serverless/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">serverless</a> design and supports multiple organizations simultaneously. <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/elasticache/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon ElastiCache Serverless</a> provides sub-millisecond response times for conversation history. <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/rds/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Relational Database Service (Amazon RDS)</a> for MySQL serves as the durable system of record. <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/firehose/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Data Firehose</a> streams usage metrics to <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/pm/serv-s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)</a> for cost management and performance optimization.</p> <h2 id="how-the-solution-works">How the solution works</h2> <p>The following diagram shows the Gallup Access AI application architecture.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/08/22/ARCHBLOG-1630-1-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram of the Gallup Access AI application showing request flow through AWS Lambda to Amazon Bedrock, with Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases and Amazon Kendra for retrieval, Amazon ElastiCache Serverless and Amazon RDS for storage, and Amazon Data Firehose streaming metrics to Amazon S3" width="800"> <p class="wp-caption-text"></p> <p>Figure 1: Gallup Access AI application architecture</p> </div> <br> The architecture processes requests through the following stages: <p></p> <p>Gallup’s proprietary workplace research covers decades of employee engagement studies, performance data, and organizational insights. The content is stored in Amazon S3 and ingested into Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases. The application also continuously crawls the Gallup website to capture the latest research publications, articles, and insights, indexing this content in Amazon Kendra for instant retrieval. This dual approach gives the AI assistant access to both historical research archives and current workplace science, delivering responses grounded in verified, up-to-date knowledge rather than generic advice.</p> <p>When a leader asks Gallup AI a question, the system retrieves relevant research from both Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases and Amazon Kendra. The system scores documents based on confidence thresholds, filters them, and consolidates them before sending them to Claude models in Amazon Bedrock.</p> <p>The conversation flows through AWS Lambda handlers that manage both real-time streaming (for web clients) and synchronous requests (for backend services). Amazon ElastiCache Serverless caches recent conversation history for instant retrieval, while Amazon RDS for MySQL serves as the durable storage layer with organized records of conversations, prompts, responses, and source citations.</p> <p>Amazon Bedrock Guardrails apply content safety policies during generation, with the ability to intervene mid-stream if policy violations are detected. Interactions persist before streaming begins, preserving transactional integrity even if connections are interrupted.</p> <p><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/systems-manager/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Systems Manager</a> Parameter Store serves as the application’s centralized configuration hub, managing AI model settings, content safety policies, and performance thresholds. This allows the team to adjust application behavior instantly, without redeploying code or interrupting service for users.</p> <p><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon DynamoDB</a> provides fast, flexible storage for product-specific insights and contextual data, so the application delivers personalized experiences tailored to each user’s role and workflow.</p> <p>Comprehensive metrics, including I/O tokens, cached tokens, time-to-first byte, and stop reasons, flow through Amazon Data Firehose to Amazon S3, providing visibility into cost, performance, and usage patterns across the application.</p> <h2 id="what-gallup-has-achieved">What Gallup has achieved</h2> <p>Gallup has transformed decades of workplace research into an intelligent assistant that delivers measurable value across thousands of organizations. Tasks that previously required navigating reports, articles, and tools now resolve through a single conversational interaction. Time to insight dropped from manual research to real-time, AI-delivered guidance within seconds. The application processes billions of tokens through production interactions, with responses grounded in verified workplace science.</p> <p>Since launching in June 2024, adoption and engagement have grown rapidly:</p> <table border="1px" cellpadding="10px" width="100%"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Metric</strong></td> <td><strong>Result</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Prompts</td> <td>Increased ~7x</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Conversations</td> <td>Increased ~4.5x</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Active users</td> <td>Increased ~5.5x</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Engagement depth</td> <td>Average prompts per conversation increased ~55%, indicating sustained, multi-turn interactions</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Response latency</td> <td>Sub-second time-to-first byte (TTFB) for streaming responses. Sub-millisecond session retrieval via Amazon ElastiCache Serverless</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <h2 id="what-the-customer-said">What the customer said</h2> <p>Gallup’s Director of Product reflects on what this shift means for how leaders access workplace science:</p> <blockquote> <p><em>“Gallup AI represents a fundamental shift in how leaders access workplace science. For decades, our research helped organizations make better decisions, but it often required leaders to search, interpret, and apply those insights themselves. By building on Amazon Bedrock, we’re embedding scientifically grounded guidance directly into the flow of work, giving managers real-time support that is both personalized and actionable.”</em></p> </blockquote> <p>— Andrew Bridger, Director of Product, Gallup</p> <p>With this foundation in place, Gallup is focused on expanding what the application can do next.</p> <h2 id="whats-next">What’s next</h2> <p>Gallup’s roadmap focuses on making its expertise more accessible, actionable, and embedded into everyday workflows. A key initiative is the development of an AI-curated prompt library that captures the most common questions managers and leaders ask. This library will help users quickly engage with Gallup AI through proven, high-value prompts grounded in workplace research.</p> <p>In addition, Gallup is introducing guided coaching experiences built around structured conversation flows. These guided prompts walk managers through well-defined coaching scenarios, such as improving engagement, addressing team challenges, or developing employees, by sequencing prompts and responses into purposeful, outcome-driven interactions.</p> <p>Gallup is building an agent-based foundation using <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</a>. This positions Gallup AI to move beyond a user-facing assistant. By surfacing tools, workflows, and proprietary knowledge programmatically, the system can support not only end users but also other systems and integrations across the application.</p> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>By combining the generative AI capabilities of <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock</a> with Gallup’s proprietary workplace research, leaders now have instant access to scientifically grounded guidance exactly when they need it. The serverless architecture enables the application to scale reliably while delivering low-latency streaming responses and comprehensive observability.</p> <p>To build your own generative AI application, get started with <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock</a>. To learn more about grounding responses in your own data, explore <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases.</a></p> <h2 id="further-reading">Further reading</h2> <ul> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/prescriptive-guidance/latest/writing-best-practices-rag/best-practices.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS prescriptive guidance: Writing best practices for RAG applications</a> : Optimize your knowledge sources for better AI retrieval and response quality.</li> <li><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-safe-generative-ai-applications-like-a-pro-best-practices-with-amazon-bedrock-guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Build safe Generative AI applications like a Pro: Best practices with Amazon Bedrock Guardrails</a> : Configure and deploy AI safety guardrails in production.</li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/generative-ai-lens/generative-ai-lens.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Well-Architected Framework, Generative AI Lens</a> : Apply proven best practices for generative AI workloads on AWS.</li> </ul> <hr> <h2>About the authors</h2>

MCP went stateless: Is your AWS MCP server deployment well-architected?

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/mcp-went-stateless-is-your-aws-mcp-server-deployment-well-architected/📅 2026-09-01 13:09 UTC

<p>On July 28, 2026, MCP published <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/changelog" target="_blank" rel="noopener">its largest revision since launch</a>, making the protocol core stateless and bringing remote MCP servers into alignment with <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/architecture/well-architected/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Well-Architected Framework</a> best practices. The <code>initialize</code> handshake is gone, and so is the <code>Mcp-Session-Id</code> header that clients had to echo on every later request. Every request now carries its own protocol version and client context. A client’s first message can be the actual tool call, and any server instance can respond to it. If your MCP server was built for the session-based protocol, the sticky sessions, shared session stores, and custom observability plumbing it required are no longer necessary. If you run behind <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-agentcore-gateway-supports-the-mcp-2026-07-28-spec/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway</a>, protocol management and backward compatibility are handled for you. This post is for teams managing the full deployment stack themselves.</p> <p>If a client wants to know what a server supports before calling it, a new <code>server/discover</code> method returns the supported protocol versions, capabilities, and identity in a single response. Servers must implement it per the <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/server/discover" target="_blank" rel="noopener">MCP 2026-07-28 specification</a>, but calling it is optional for the client.</p> <p>This matters on AWS because the old design fought horizontal scaling. A session lived on whichever instance issued it. Running more than one instance meant either pinning clients with sticky routing or externalizing session state to a shared store. Both were correct for that protocol. With the new protocol, neither is required. This post maps the <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28" target="_blank" rel="noopener">MCP 2026-07-28 specification</a> against the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentic-ai-lens.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Well-Architected Agentic AI Lens</a> and recommends migrating, because the new protocol achieves natively what the old one could only achieve through compensating infrastructure.</p> <p>One thing to settle up front, because it drives everything else: stateless describes the protocol, not your application. Stateful use cases still work.</p> <p>Think of it as a coat check. Under the old protocol the server was a valet who remembered your face, which meant you had to keep dealing with that same valet and nobody else could help you. Now you get a numbered ticket, and any attendant can serve you because the ticket carries the reference. When a server needs continuity across calls, a tool returns an identifier for the stored state. The model includes that identifier on the calls that follow. The state stays in your datastore. The model carries only the key. This is ordinary REST discipline. It has an advantage over the old model. The identifier sits in the model’s context rather than hidden in a header. The model can reason about it and thread it across tools.</p> <h2 id="what-changes-in-your-architecture">What changes in your architecture</h2> <p>The following table compares the deployment patterns the session-based protocol required against the patterns the stateless core now supports.</p> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Before (session-based)</strong></td> <td><strong>After (2026-07-28 stateless)</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Elastic Load Balancing Application Load Balancer (ALB) stickiness so each session reaches the same instance.</td> <td>Plain round-robin. Delete the stickiness configuration.</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Session state in Amazon DynamoDB or Amazon ElastiCache.</td> <td>No session store. Server-minted identifiers passed as tool arguments.</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Parse request bodies at the gateway to route by method.</td> <td>Route and throttle on the <code>Mcp-Method</code> and <code>Mcp-Name</code> headers.</td> </tr> <tr> <td>AWS Lambda required workarounds for the stateful handshake.</td> <td>AWS Lambda is a natural fit. Request in, response out.</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Refetch tool lists per session. No caching story.</td> <td>Cache with <code>ttlMs</code> and <code>cacheScope</code>, the protocol’s built-in freshness fields.</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Bolt-on tracing per implementation. Proprietary protocol logging channel.</td> <td>W3C Trace Context in <code>_meta</code> for distributed tracing. <code>stderr</code> or OpenTelemetry for logging. Protocol logging is deprecated.</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Rely on stream resumption (<code>Last-Event-ID</code>) for broken responses.</td> <td>Make tools idempotent. Clients re-issue broken calls.</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <p><strong><img src="https://s.w.org/images/core/emoji/14.0.0/72x72/26a0.png" alt="⚠" class="wp-smiley" style="height: 1em; max-height: 1em;"> Don’t delete yet if you serve 2025-era clients</strong>. The 2026-07-28 spec includes a backward-compatible lane that preserves session semantics for older clients. Your ALB stickiness rules and session store (DynamoDB/ElastiCache) <strong>must remain in place</strong> until you stop serving pre-2026-07-28 clients. <br> <strong>Action:</strong> Instrument your gateway to log protocol version per request. Set a sunset date for the legacy lane and communicate it to client teams. Only decommission session infrastructure after traffic on the old version reaches zero. This guidance applies to session infrastructure built to compensate for the old protocol’s requirements. Managed hosts that offer session features by design for specific use cases are not in scope.</p> <p><strong>One behavioral change to plan for.</strong> Servers can no longer push a request to a client mid-call, which is how confirmations, sampling, and root queries used to work over a held-open stream. The spec replaces that pattern with <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/patterns/mrtr" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Multi Round-Trip Requests (MRTR)</a>. A server that needs input returns an <code>input_required</code> result containing an <code>inputRequests</code> map. This map holds elicitations, sampling calls, or root queries, and an opaque <code>requestState</code> token. The client fulfills the requests, then re-sends the original call with <code>inputResponses</code> and the echoed <code>requestState</code>. Any instance can pick that up because <code>requestState</code> carries all the context the server needs to resume. No shared session store is required. The server does not hold the connection open. This is what makes the pattern work on AWS Lambda.</p> <h2 id="the-well-architected-view">The Well-Architected view</h2> <p>The <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Well-Architected Agentic AI Lens</a> already prescribes standardized protocol-based integration as a best practice. For more detail, refer to <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentops04-bp02.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Establish standardized tool integration protocols (MCP, A2A)</a>. What follows is not new guidance but a reading of how the <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28" target="_blank" rel="noopener">MCP 2026-07-28 specification</a> makes those best practices genuinely achievable for a remote MCP server, pillar by pillar.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/08/27/ARCHBLOG-1666-1.png" alt="Diagram mapping MCP 2026-07-28 protocol changes to the six Well-Architected Agentic AI Lens pillars" width="800"> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 1: How the MCP 2026-07-28 specification maps to the Well-Architected Agentic AI Lens pillars</p> </div> <p><strong>Operational excellence.</strong> The Lens identifies observability as the foundation for operating agents. If you cannot trace a decision end to end, you cannot debug, optimize, or audit it. The 2026-07-28 spec builds observability into the protocol itself. Three changes make this concrete:</p> <ol type="1"> <li><strong>Tracing.</strong> Every request carries W3C Trace Context keys in <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic#_meta" target="_blank" rel="noopener">_meta</a> (<code>traceparent</code>, <code>tracestate</code>, <code>baggage</code>), so it traces end to end through any OpenTelemetry-compatible backend, including Amazon CloudWatch. The Lens prescribes <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentops05-bp01.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">end-to-end tracing and telemetry for agent operations</a>.</li> <li><strong>Operational signals without body parsing.</strong> The <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/transports/streamable-http#request-metadata" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Mcp-Method and Mcp-Name headers</a> expose the operation type on every POST, and every response carries a required <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic#result-responses" target="_blank" rel="noopener">resultType field</a> (<code>complete</code> or <code>input_required</code>). Gateways and observability tools get unambiguous per-operation signals for metrics, alarms, and AWS WAF rules without inspecting payloads. The result directly addresses the Lens recommendation for <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentops05-bp02.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">implementing metrics and monitoring for agent-specific patterns</a>.</li> <li><strong>Standardized logging.</strong> MCP’s proprietary protocol logging is <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/deprecated#deprecated" target="_blank" rel="noopener">deprecated</a> in favor of <code>stderr</code> and OpenTelemetry. The Lens makes the same recommendation: <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentops05-bp03.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">implement structured logging through standardized, queryable formats</a>.</li> </ol> <p><strong>Security.</strong> The Lens treats agent security as harder than traditional service security: agents act autonomously with delegated credentials, and their inputs (including state identifiers) are visible to, and potentially manipulable by, the model. MCP’s 2026-07-28 spec hardens the protocol surface against these risks. Five changes strengthen the security posture:</p> <ol type="1"> <li><strong>Issuer validation.</strong> Clients must validate the <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/authorization#authorization-response-validation" target="_blank" rel="noopener">iss parameter per RFC 9207</a>, confirming which authorization server produced a response. The Lens calls for the same discipline under <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentsec03-bp01.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">strong authentication for agent identities</a>.</li> <li><strong>Client type declaration.</strong> Clients must declare <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/authorization/client-registration" target="_blank" rel="noopener">application_type at registration</a> so a desktop or CLI client is not mistaken for a web app, verifying authentication mechanisms match the client’s security profile. The same Lens best practice applies: <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentsec03-bp01.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">strong authentication for agent identities</a>. (Note: Dynamic Client Registration itself is <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/deprecated#deprecated" target="_blank" rel="noopener">now deprecated</a> in favor of Client ID Metadata Documents.)</li> <li><strong>Bounded human interaction.</strong> A server can prompt a user only while it is handling that user’s request, through the <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/patterns/mrtr#multi-round-trip-requests" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Multi Round-Trip Requests</a> pattern. This is a protocol-enforced constraint that bounds when human interaction can occur, aligning with the Lens’s <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentsec04-bp02.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">human-in-the-loop controls for critical decisions</a>.</li> <li><strong>Ownership enforcement.</strong> Because state identifiers are visible to the model, servers must enforce ownership on every call. The protocol will not stop a caller from presenting an identifier that is not theirs, so the Lens best practice for <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentsec02-bp01.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">tool authorization at the gateway</a> applies: validate that the requesting identity owns the resource it references. The same discipline applies to <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/patterns/mrtr#security-considerations" target="_blank" rel="noopener">requestState tokens</a>: the spec requires servers to treat them as untrusted input and protect their integrity with HMAC or AEAD, rejecting any token that fails verification.</li> <li><strong>Schema validation.</strong> Tool input and output schemas are now validated against <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic#json-schema-usage" target="_blank" rel="noopener">JSON Schema 2020-12</a>, giving servers a formal contract for rejecting malformed or injected arguments before execution. This maps to the Lens requirement to <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentsec02-bp02.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">validating tool inputs at the boundary</a>.</li> </ol> <p><strong>Reliability.</strong> Agents hold multi-step context that is expensive to reconstruct after failure, making reliability harder than in traditional services. MCP’s 2026-07-28 spec addresses this at the protocol layer. Four changes reduce that fragility:</p> <ol type="1"> <li><strong>Stateless transport.</strong> The spec <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic#statelessness" target="_blank" rel="noopener">removes protocol-level sessions</a>, so any instance can serve any request. Instance loss is a non-event. Retries need no session affinity, and scale-in never drains sessions. The protocol embodies the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentrel01-bp01.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">failure-isolation philosophy</a> at the protocol layer without additional infrastructure.</li> <li><strong>Continuation tokens.</strong> Interrupted multi-step interactions resume through <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/patterns/mrtr#inputrequiredresult" target="_blank" rel="noopener">requestState</a>, an opaque continuation token the server returns and the client echoes on retry. This embodies the Lens principle of <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentrel07-bp01.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">designing workflows in stages with incremental recovery</a>.</li> <li><strong>Idempotent retry.</strong> <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/transports/streamable-http#sending-messages" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Stream resumability was removed</a>, so a broken response stream loses the in-flight payload and the client must re-issue the call. The mitigation is the same <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentrel06-bp04.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">idempotent task execution pattern</a> the Lens prescribes for retryable agent actions: make tools idempotent so re-issued requests produce no duplicate side effects.</li> <li><strong>Standardized error codes.</strong> The spec <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic#error-codes" target="_blank" rel="noopener">allocates error code ranges</a> (-32000 to -32019 implementation-defined, -32020 to -32099 reserved for MCP), giving clients and gateways a canonical signal set for retry, backoff, and circuit-breaking decisions. Gateways can now implement <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentrel01-bp04.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">standardized communication protocols</a>.</li> </ol> <p><strong>Performance efficiency.</strong> Redundant data fetches and per-interaction protocol overhead are the two main performance drags the Lens identifies in agentic workloads. MCP’s 2026-07-28 spec addresses both at the protocol layer. Three changes reduce that overhead:</p> <ol type="1"> <li><strong>Protocol-declared caching.</strong> Two fields are <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/server/utilities/caching#cacheable-model" target="_blank" rel="noopener">now required on list and resource-read results</a>: <code>ttlMs</code> (how many milliseconds a response stays fresh) and <code>cacheScope</code> (whether shared intermediaries can cache it or only the requesting client). <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/server/tools#listing-tools" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Tool lists now return in deterministic order</a>, allowing LLM prompt-cache hits across calls. The protocol now delivers what the Lens recommends under <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentperf06-bp01.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">optimizing inference-time performance for agent workloads</a>.</li> <li><strong>Freshness semantics.</strong> Clients and MCP-aware gateways can <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/server/utilities/caching#time-to-live-ttl-field" target="_blank" rel="noopener">cache responses using protocol-declared freshness</a> (<code>ttlMs</code> + <code>cacheScope</code>), the same data-type-specific TTL discipline the Lens recommends under <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentperf03-bp04.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">protocol-declared freshness semantics</a>, without guessing at staleness.</li> <li><strong>Header-based routing.</strong> <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/transports/streamable-http#request-metadata" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Routing and throttling decisions</a> now live in HTTP headers (<code>Mcp-Method</code>, <code>Mcp-Name</code>) rather than parsed message bodies, reducing per-interaction overhead in line with what the Lens prescribes for <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentperf04-bp02.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">efficient protocol-based agent communications</a>.</li> </ol> <p><strong>Cost optimization.</strong> The Lens identifies always-on infrastructure serving bursty agent traffic as the highest source of idle cost in an agent stack. MCP’s <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic#statelessness" target="_blank" rel="noopener">stateless architecture</a> eliminates an entire category of that cost: session infrastructure.</p> <ol type="1"> <li><strong>Delete session infrastructure.</strong> Audit for anything that exists only to preserve sessions (ElastiCache clusters, sticky-routing rules, session-replication logic) and delete it. This follows the same principle the Lens applies to <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentcost04-bp02.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">cost-optimizing tool serving through serverless and resource sharing</a>. Infrastructure that runs constantly to serve unpredictable traffic should be replaced with consumption-based patterns that scale to zero. A two-node Amazon ElastiCache (cache.t4g.micro) session store is about $23/month (<a href="https://calculator.aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Pricing Calculator, July 2026</a>). The larger saving is eliminating an entire class of infrastructure and the operational burden around it. Sticky routing costs capacity too by distributing load unevenly, and the savings scale with the size of your fleet.</li> <li><strong>Serverless as first-class pattern.</strong> AWS Lambda has no sticky routing and no persistent connections. A session-based MCP server meant externalizing state to a shared store. Even a “session-free” mode still paid for the mandatory handshake. With the <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic#statelessness" target="_blank" rel="noopener">2026-07-28 stateless core</a>, request in, response out is exactly what AWS Lambda does natively. Serverless MCP moves from workaround to first-class pattern, delivering what the Lens recommends for <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentcost04-bp02.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">cost-optimizing tool serving through serverless and resource sharing</a>.</li> </ol> <p><strong>Sustainability.</strong> The Lens identifies static provisioning for bursty agent traffic as the primary source of wasted infrastructure capacity. The <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic#statelessness" target="_blank" rel="noopener">2026-07-28 spec’s stateless architecture</a> eliminates the structural reasons for that over-provisioning.</p> <ol type="1"> <li><strong>No more pinned-session capacity.</strong> The spec’s <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic#statelessness" target="_blank" rel="noopener">stateless design</a> means no instance holds a session, so no instance needs to stay warm for one. Right-size against your actual traffic pattern rather than a theoretical peak, the same principle the Lens applies to <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentsus02-bp03.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">appropriately scaling compute, networking, and data dependencies</a> for agent workloads. Instance-agnostic routing means the fleet you do keep can run closer to its real utilization, instead of padding for the instances that happened to hold long-lived sessions.</li> </ol> <p>The <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Well-Architected Agentic AI Lens</a> articulated these best practices as general principles for agentic workloads. The fact that a major protocol revision, designed independently, converges on the same architectural shape is evidence that the framework captures something real about how reliable distributed systems need to work.</p> <h2 id="what-to-watch">What to watch</h2> <p>The architectural shift creates its own operational surface. These are the areas where the new defaults need deliberate attention rather than passive adoption.</p> <p><strong>Long-lived streams did not disappear.</strong> The <code>subscriptions/listen</code> method consolidates change notifications into a single opt-in POST-response stream, so check idle timeouts across your load balancer, proxy, and compute tier if your servers use it.</p> <p><strong>Deprecations with a clock.</strong> The spec deprecated Roots, Sampling, Logging, and the HTTP+SSE transport with a <a href="https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/2026-07-28-release-candidate/#roots-sampling-and-logging-are-deprecated" target="_blank" rel="noopener">twelve-month floor</a> before removal. The earliest any of these can be removed is July 2027. It also removed <code>ping</code>, <code>logging/setLevel</code>, and <code>notifications/roots/list_changed</code> outright, and moved log level into per-request <code>_meta</code>. The suggested migration paths:</p> <ul> <li>Pass directories through tool parameters or resource URIs instead of Roots.</li> <li>Integrate directly with LLM provider APIs instead of Sampling.</li> <li>Log to <code>stderr</code> or OpenTelemetry instead of protocol-level Logging.</li> <li>Migrate HTTP+SSE to Streamable HTTP.</li> </ul> <p>Plan the exits now rather than at the deadline.</p> <p><strong><a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/seps/1865-mcp-apps-interactive-user-interfaces-for-mcp" target="_blank" rel="noopener">MCP Apps</a> puts server-supplied HTML inside your host.</strong> Pre-declared UI resource templates, mandatory <code>iframe</code> sandboxing, and auditable JSON-RPC communication between the <code>iframe</code> and host all help. But treat template review as mandatory before deployment, and decide deliberately which servers in your fleet can ship UI at all.</p> <p><strong>cacheScope is a multi-tenant disclosure risk.</strong> Setting <code>cacheScope: "public"</code> on a response that contains tenant-specific data lets shared intermediaries serve one tenant’s list to another. Default to <code>"private"</code> and widen deliberately only for responses that are genuinely identical across callers.</p> <h2 id="built-in-protection-against-future-breaks">Built-in protection against future breaks</h2> <p>Three mechanisms shipped alongside the stateless core to prevent a repeat of this kind of breaking change.</p> <p>A <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/community/feature-lifecycle" target="_blank" rel="noopener">feature lifecycle policy</a> gives every feature an Active, Deprecated, or Removed state. Nothing can be removed until at least twelve months after it is deprecated. An extensions framework lets new capabilities ship as opt-in extensions that prove themselves outside the core. That is where Tasks landed after its experimental version <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/seps/2663-tasks-extension" target="_blank" rel="noopener">needed a redesign</a>. And no Standards Track proposal can reach Final status without a matching scenario in the <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/seps/2484-conformance-tests-required-for-final-seps" target="_blank" rel="noopener">conformance suite</a>. This is the same suite the official SDKs are validated against.</p> <p>The handshake and session removal were a deliberate, one-time break to fix the foundation. From here, what you build against 2026-07-28 comes with documented notice periods.</p> <h2 id="self-check">Self-check</h2> <p>Run these ten questions against your own deployment before you decide whether, and how, to migrate.</p> <ol type="1"> <li>Can any instance of your server handle any request, with no session affinity at the load balancer?</li> <li>Have you deleted everything that existed only to preserve a protocol session?</li> <li>Do your list responses set <code>ttlMs</code> and <code>cacheScope</code> deliberately, and does your gateway route on headers rather than parsed bodies?</li> <li>Does every client validate <code>iss</code>, and does every server enforce ownership per identifier rather than trusting the identifier itself?</li> <li>Do you have a firm date to stop supporting 2025-11-25 clients?</li> <li>Have you replaced server-initiated pushes with Multi Round-Trip Requests so no instance holds a connection open for client input?</li> <li>Are your tools idempotent so clients can safely re-issue any broken call?</li> <li>Do you propagate W3C Trace Context end-to-end and emit logs through <code>stderr</code> or OpenTelemetry instead of MCP protocol logging?</li> <li>Are you still paying for session infrastructure (DynamoDB, ElastiCache, sticky routing) that nothing uses?</li> <li>Do you have a governance policy for MCP Apps before any server in your fleet exposes one?</li> </ol> <p>A “no” to any of these is where the new spec pays off. Each maps to the pillar sections earlier in this post. Start with the migration path that follows, run your server against the official conformance suite, and use the related AWS resources at the end to plan the change.</p> <h2 id="migration-path">Migration path</h2> <p>You do not need to move immediately. Protocol versions are frozen snapshots, and a client and server only need to share one, so 2025-11-25 servers keep working with clients that still speak it. But hosts retire old versions on their own timeline, the community is already moving (GitHub’s MCP Server <a href="https://github.blog/changelog/2026-07-23-github-mcp-server-supports-the-next-mcp-specification/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">shipped support ahead of the release</a>), and 2025-11-25 is now frozen. Future capabilities and fixes land on 2026-07-28 or later.</p> <p><strong>For a new server,</strong> target 2026-07-28 directly: stateless from the start, explicit identifiers, and no dependence on Roots, Sampling, or MCP Logging.</p> <p><strong>For an existing server,</strong> work through these steps in order:</p> <ol type="1"> <li><strong>Upgrade the SDK and <a href="https://ts.sdk.modelcontextprotocol.io/v2/migration/support-2026-07-28" target="_blank" rel="noopener">opt in</a>.</strong> Speaking the new revision is never automatic.</li> <li><strong>Audit for session assumptions</strong> and migrate off the experimental Tasks API if you used it (Tasks is now an <a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/seps/2663-tasks-extension" target="_blank" rel="noopener">official extension</a> with a redesigned interface).</li> <li><strong>Plan the deprecation exits</strong> (Roots, Sampling, Logging, HTTP+SSE) and change the resource-not-found error code from <code>-32002</code> to <code>-32602</code>.</li> <li><strong>Collect the infrastructure savings</strong> by deleting session stores, sticky-routing rules, and handshake infrastructure.</li> </ol> <p><strong>For a platform or gateway team:</strong> add header-based routing and per-operation throttling on <code>Mcp-Method</code>, honor <code>ttlMs</code> and <code>cacheScope</code> in your caching layer. Also propagate W3C Trace Context, and set a policy for MCP Apps before the first server in your fleet ships one.</p> <p><strong>Validate before you ship.</strong> The <a href="https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/conformance" target="_blank" rel="noopener">official conformance suite</a> covers the new behaviors, and protocol inspectors can pin 2026-07-28 to test your server against exactly what clients will send. Start in a test environment, then promote to production once the suite passes.</p> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>The session-based protocol was correct for the constraints it operated under, but those constraints are gone. If you are deploying MCP servers on AWS, the 2026-07-28 specification is the Well-Architected path forward. Migrate your servers, sunset your legacy lane, and delete the infrastructure that existed only to compensate for a protocol limitation that no longer applies.</p> <hr> <h2>About the authors</h2>
